.

We first remark that we have excluded both
Li (2018)
and
Li and Lin (2024)
with spatial weight matrix
\mathbf{W}_{2}
, as their in-sample errors explode. This may be due to (near) non-stationarity, as large errors are also observed in Li-
\mathbf{W}_{1}
, Li-
\mathbf{W}_{3}
, Li & Lin-
\mathbf{W}_{1}
, and Li & Lin-
\mathbf{W}_{3}
, suggesting that more sophisticated spatial weight configurations may be necessary for their models to perform adequately.

Overall, both DSAR-1 and DSAR-2 models perform very well across all five performance measures. In particular, DSAR-1, which uses three different spatial weight matrices, achieves the best predictive power, while DSAR-2 attains smaller in-sample errors. Notably, our DSAR-1 and DSAR-2 models achieve the highest Sharpe ratios among all models. We observe that
Lam and Souza (2020)
and
Liang et al. (2022)
, which use a sparse correction matrix and semiparametric spatial weights respectively, can achieve comparable or slightly better performance. However, both approaches come with much higher computational complexity, being over 500 times slower than our model fitting. This again highlights the advantage of our proposed approach: achieving strong statistical performance with low computational cost.

7
Conclusions

In this work, we advance dynamic spatial modeling by introducing a framework that enables data-driven selection of time-varying spatial structures, addressing a critical gap in the spatial econometrics literature. By integrating adaptive penalization with instrumental variables, our framework and method provides practitioners with a principled approach for resolving uncertainty in the specification of spatial weight matrices while detecting structural breaks. Empirical studies demonstrate that our method achieves strong statistical performance at a substantially lower computational cost compared with existing models.

A promising direction for future research is to extend our framework to incorporate time-varying regression coefficients or even spatial autoregressive terms using lagged panels, which would further enhance flexibility. In applied econometrics, our method can provide new insights into evolving spatial systems, with applications ranging from financial contagion to climate risk propagation.

Supplement to “Inference on Dynamic Spatial Autoregressive Models with Change Point Detection”

In this supplementary material, we provide additional mathematical details for Section
2.2
in the main text, extra simulation results, and additional explanations for the real data analysis. Proofs of the main results stated in the main text are also included, together with auxiliary results and their proofs.

S1
Additional details for the model

S1.1
Additional details for Section
2.2

Together with (
2.11
), the least squares problem in (
2.6
) can be described as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\phi}}

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\bm{\phi}}\;\frac{1}{2T}\Big\|\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{V}\bm{\phi}-\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{\bm{\beta}(\bm{\phi})}\vec{\mathbf{I}_{d}}\Big\|^{2}.

(S1)

With the least squares estimator
\widetilde{\bm{\phi}}
, the problem in (
2.7
) in matrix notation is

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\phi}}

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\bm{\phi}}\;\frac{1}{2T}\Big\|\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{V}\bm{\phi}-\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{\bm{\beta}(\bm{\phi})}\vec{\mathbf{I}_{d}}\Big\|^{2}+\lambda\mathbf{u}^{\top}|\bm{\phi}|,

(S2)

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\text{subj. to}\;\;\;\|\bm{\Lambda}_{t}\bm{\Phi}\|_{\infty}<1,\;\;\;\text{with}\;\;\;|\mathbf{z}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\phi}|<1.

Note the squared errors in (
S1
) and (
S2
) are still implicit in
\bm{\phi}
due to
\bm{\beta}(\bm{\phi})
.

Note from (
2.13
) and the definition of
\mathbf{B}
in (
2.9
), we have

\displaystyle\hskip 12.0pt\mathbf{B}^{\top}\Big(\mathbf{I}_{d}\otimes\Big\{\Big(\mathbf{I}_{T}\otimes\Big\{(\mathbf{y}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big[\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big]^{-1}\Big\}\Big)(\mathbf{X}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{X}_{T})^{\top}\Big\}\Big)\vec{\mathbf{I}_{d}}

\displaystyle=T^{-1/2}d^{-a/2}\Big(\mathbf{I}_{d}\otimes\Big\{(\mathbf{B}_{1}-\bar{\mathbf{B}},\dots,\mathbf{B}_{T}-\bar{\mathbf{B}})(\mathbf{I}_{T}\otimes\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle\hskip 12.0pt\Big(\mathbf{I}_{T}\otimes\Big\{(\mathbf{y}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big[\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big]^{-1}\Big\}\Big)(\mathbf{X}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{X}_{T})^{\top}\Big\}\Big)\vec{\mathbf{I}_{d}}

\displaystyle=T^{-1/2}d^{-a/2}\Big(\mathbf{I}_{d}\otimes\Big\{\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\mathbf{B}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{B}})\bm{\gamma}(\mathbf{y}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big[\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big]^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\top}\Big\}\Big)\vec{\mathbf{I}_{d}}

\displaystyle=T^{-1/2}d^{-a/2}\vec{\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\mathbf{B}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{B}})\bm{\gamma}(\mathbf{y}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big[\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big]^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\top}}

\displaystyle=T^{-1/2}d^{-a/2}\Big(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{X}_{t}\otimes(\mathbf{B}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{B}})\bm{\gamma}\Big)\vec{(\mathbf{y}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big[\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big]^{-1}}

\displaystyle=T^{-1/2}d^{-a/2}\Big(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{X}_{t}\otimes(\mathbf{B}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{B}})\bm{\gamma}\Big)\big[\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big]^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{y}^{\nu}.

Similarly, by the definition of
\mathbf{Y}_{W}
,

\displaystyle\hskip 12.0pt\mathbf{B}^{\top}\Big(\mathbf{I}_{d}\otimes\Big\{\Big(\mathbf{I}_{T}\otimes\Big\{\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{p}\sum_{k=0}^{l_{j}}\phi_{j,k}(\mathbf{y}_{j,k}^{\nu})^{\top}(\mathbf{W}_{j}^{\otimes})^{\top}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 36.0pt\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big[\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big]^{-1}\Big\}\Big)(\mathbf{X}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{X}_{T})^{\top}\Big\}\Big)\vec{\mathbf{I}_{d}}

\displaystyle=T^{-1/2}d^{-a/2}\Big(\mathbf{I}_{d}\otimes\Big\{(\mathbf{B}_{1}-\bar{\mathbf{B}},\dots,\mathbf{B}_{T}-\bar{\mathbf{B}})(\mathbf{I}_{T}\otimes\bm{\gamma})

\displaystyle\hskip 12.0pt\cdot\Big(\mathbf{I}_{T}\otimes\Big\{\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{p}\sum_{k=0}^{l_{j}}\phi_{j,k}(\mathbf{y}_{j,k}^{\nu})^{\top}(\mathbf{W}_{j}^{\otimes})^{\top}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 12.0pt\cdot\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big[\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big]^{-1}\Big\}\Big)(\mathbf{X}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{X}_{T})^{\top}\Big\}\Big)\vec{\mathbf{I}_{d}}

\displaystyle=T^{-1/2}d^{-a/2}\Big(\mathbf{I}_{d}\otimes\Big\{\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\mathbf{B}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{B}})\bm{\gamma}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{p}\sum_{k=0}^{l_{j}}\phi_{j,k}(\mathbf{y}_{j,k}^{\nu})^{\top}(\mathbf{W}_{j}^{\otimes})^{\top}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 12.0pt\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big[\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big]^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\top}\Big\}\Big)\vec{\mathbf{I}_{d}}

\displaystyle=T^{-1/2}d^{-a/2}\vec{\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\mathbf{B}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{B}})\bm{\gamma}(\bm{\phi}^{\top}\mathbf{Y}_{W}^{\top})\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big[\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big]^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\top}}

\displaystyle=T^{-1/2}d^{-a/2}\Big(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{X}_{t}\otimes(\mathbf{B}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{B}})\bm{\gamma}\Big)\big[\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big]^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{Y}_{W}\bm{\phi}.

With (
2.13
), the term inside the squared Euclidean norm of (
S1
) can hence be further written as

\begin{split}&\hskip 12.0pt\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{V}\bm{\phi}-\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{\bm{\beta}(\bm{\phi})}\vec{\mathbf{I}_{d}}\\
&=\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{V}\bm{\phi}-\mathbf{B}^{\top}\Big(\mathbf{I}_{d}\otimes\Big\{\Big(\mathbf{I}_{T}\otimes\Big\{(\mathbf{y}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big[\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big]^{-1}\Big\}\Big)\\
&\hskip 12.0pt\cdot\big(\mathbf{X}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{X}_{T}\big)^{\top}\Big\}\Big)\vec{\mathbf{I}_{d}}\\
&\hskip 12.0pt+\mathbf{B}^{\top}\Big(\mathbf{I}_{d}\otimes\Big\{\Big(\mathbf{I}_{T}\otimes\Big\{\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{p}\sum_{k=0}^{l_{j}}\phi_{j,k}(\mathbf{y}_{j,k}^{\nu})^{\top}(\mathbf{W}_{j}^{\otimes})^{\top}\Big)\\
&\hskip 25.0pt\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big[\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big]^{-1}\Big\}\Big)(\mathbf{X}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{X}_{T})^{\top}\Big\}\Big)\vec{\mathbf{I}_{d}}\\
&=\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{y}-T^{-1/2}d^{-a/2}\Big(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{X}_{t}\otimes(\mathbf{B}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{B}})\bm{\gamma}\Big)\big[\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big]^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{y}^{\nu}\\
&-\Big\{\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{V}-T^{-1/2}d^{-a/2}\Big(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{X}_{t}\otimes(\mathbf{B}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{B}})\bm{\gamma}\Big)\big[\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{X}\big]^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\mathbf{Y}_{W}\Big\}\bm{\phi}\\
&=\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{y}-\bm{\Xi}\mathbf{y}^{\nu}-(\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{V}-\bm{\Xi}\mathbf{Y}_{W})\bm{\phi}.\end{split}

(S3)

S1.2
Identification of the model (
2.11
)

We show that the coefficients
\bm{\phi}^{\ast}
and
\bm{\beta}^{\ast}
in model (
2.11
) are identified under Assumption (I1). Assume that the two sets of parameters
(\check{\bm{\phi}},\check{\bm{\beta}})
and
(\acute{\bm{\phi}},\acute{\bm{\beta}})
both satisfy model (
2.11
), then we have

\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{V}\check{\bm{\phi}}+\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{\check{\bm{\beta}}}\vec{\mathbf{I}_{d}}=\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{V}\acute{\bm{\phi}}+\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{\acute{\bm{\beta}}}\vec{\mathbf{I}_{d}}.

By noticing that
\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{\bm{\beta}}\vec{\mathbf{I}_{d}}=\mathbf{B}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}\bm{\beta}
, we may rearrange the above and arrive at

\mathbf{0}=\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{V}(\check{\bm{\phi}}-\acute{\bm{\phi}})+\mathbf{B}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}(\check{\bm{\beta}}-\acute{\bm{\beta}})=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{V}&\mathbf{B}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\check{\bm{\phi}}-\acute{\bm{\phi}}\\
\check{\bm{\beta}}-\acute{\bm{\beta}}\end{pmatrix}.

Taking expectation and left-multiplying by
(\mathbf{Q}^{\top}\mathbf{Q})^{-1}\mathbf{Q}^{\top}
on both sides, we obtain
\check{\bm{\phi}}=\acute{\bm{\phi}}
and
\check{\bm{\beta}}=\acute{\bm{\beta}}
. This implies
\bm{\phi}^{\ast}
and
\bm{\beta}^{\ast}
are identified in model (
2.1
), and hence
\bm{\mu}^{\ast}
is uniquely identified.

It is worth to remind that in practice, we should be careful in constructing dynamic variables to due to the issue of multicollinearity, as reflected in
\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{V}
above.

S1.3
Additional details to Section
5.1

In this subsection, we discuss the model framework with regime changes based on multiple threshold variables with multiple threshold values. For illustrations, consider the following spatial autoregressive model with
(k+1)
regimes, where
k
can be unknown:

\mathbf{y}_{t}=\bm{\mu}^{\ast}+\Big(\phi_{1}^{\ast}\mathbbm{1}\{q_{t}\leq\gamma_{1}^{\ast}\}+\phi_{2}^{\ast}\mathbbm{1}\{\gamma_{1}^{\ast}<q_{t}\leq\gamma_{2}^{\ast}\}+\dots+\phi_{k}^{\ast}\mathbbm{1}\{q_{t}>\gamma_{k}^{\ast}\}\Big)\mathbf{W}_{1}\mathbf{y}_{t}+\mathbf{X}_{t}\bm{\beta}^{\ast}+\bm{\epsilon}_{t}.

(S4)

Model (
S4
) can be written in the form of (
5.5
), with the consistency of
\{\phi_{1,l}^{\ast}\}_{l\in[L]}
guaranteed by Corollary
4
. This also implies that the estimation on
k
is consistent.

Corollary 4

(Consistency on the number of threshold regimes and multiple threshold values estimation)
Let all assumptions in Theorem
2
hold. For model (
S4
), let
\{\widehat{\phi}_{1,l}\}_{l\in[L]}
denote the adaptive LASSO solution for
\{\phi_{1,l}^{\ast}\}_{l\in[L]}
in (
5.5
). Then,
\widehat{k}:=|\{\gamma_{l}:\widehat{\phi}_{1,l}\neq 0\}|
estimates
k
consistently. Moreover, for every
i\in[\widehat{k}]
, the
i
-th smallest element in
\{\gamma_{l}:\widehat{\phi}_{1,l}\neq 0\}
estimates
\gamma_{i}^{\ast}
consistently.

As we often have limited prior knowledge on the parameter space in practice, we recommend using our framework in an exploratory way. This should help researchers discover more reasonable threshold structures in the data.

S2
Additional simulations

S2.1
Different methods to select the tuning parameter

In this subsection, we conduct Monte Carlo experiments to study the sensitivity of the estimator’s performance with respect to different methods for selecting the tuning parameter
\lambda
in the adaptive LASSO problem. Specifically, we compare our BIC criterion in (
4.1
) with two alternative choices: (i) three-fold cross validation minimizing the least squares error, which effectively uses the first term in (
4.1
) as the criterion (five-fold and ten-fold cross validation are also experimented, yielding similar results and thus omitted here); (ii) the BIC criterion in (
4.2
), with variables constructed under the model framework (
2.1
), which is a reasonable choice when
L
is small.

We use a similar data generating process to (
6.1
):

\mathbf{y}_{t}=\Big\{\mathbf{I}_{d}-\big(0+0.2\,z_{1,1,t}+0\,z_{1,2,t}\big)\mathbf{W}_{1}-\big(0+0.2\,z_{2,1,t}+0\,z_{2,2,t}\big)\mathbf{W}_{2}\Big\}^{-1}\big(\bm{\mu}^{\ast}+\mathbf{X}_{t}\bm{\beta}^{\ast}+\bm{\epsilon}_{t}\big),

(S1)

where
\{z_{1,1,t}\}
,
\{z_{1,2,t}\}
,
\{z_{2,1,t}\}
,
\{z_{2,2,t}\}
,
\bm{\mu}^{\ast}
,
\mathbf{X}_{t}
,
\bm{\beta}^{\ast}
,
\mathbf{W}_{1}
,
\mathbf{W}_{2}
, and
\bm{\epsilon}_{t}
are generated in the same way as described after (
6.1
) in the main text, and
z_{1,0,t}=z_{2,0,t}=1
for all
t\in[T]
. We first fit the model using these dynamic variables, i.e.,
L=6
. For a more thorough comparison between the two criteria in (
4.1
) and (
4.2
), we also perform estimation using additional dynamic variables
\{z_{1,3,t}\},\dots,\{z_{1,9,t}\},\{z_{2,3,t}\},\dots,\{z_{2,9,t}\}
, all generated independently from
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. In this case, we have that
L=20
and that the true parameters corresponding to these extra dynamic variables are all zero. For both scenarios, we experiment with
T=50
and
d=25,50
, respectively, and each setting is repeated 500 times. Table
S1
reports the error measures of the parameter estimators for all settings under
L=6
, and Table
S2
for
L=20

Both tables show that selecting
\lambda
using either of the two BIC criteria greatly outperforms selection based on the least squares criterion (“CV”) in terms of specificity, while all three methods achieve high sensitivity. This is reasonable, as the BIC criteria are specifically designed to address model overfitting and are thus less likely to select truly zero parameters. We also observe that the results under the two BIC criteria are very similar, which is expected since even
L=20
in Table
S2
is still considered small. The improvement of our proposed criterion “BIC2” over “BIC” is mild, but both tables demonstrate the strength of our approach and suggest that “BIC” may also be used effectively when
L
is not large.

S2.2
Sensitivity of parameter estimators to endogeneity

In the main text, we demonstrated the effectiveness of our parameter estimators under mild endogeneity. To further investigate the impact of endogeneity, we consider the model

\mathbf{y}_{t}=\Big\{\mathbf{I}_{d}-\big(0+0.2\,z_{1,1,t}+0\,z_{1,2,t}\big)\mathbf{W}_{1}-\big(0+0.2\,z_{2,1,t}+0\,z_{2,2,t}\big)\mathbf{W}_{2}\Big\}^{-1}\big(\bm{\mu}^{\ast}+\mathbf{X}_{t}\bm{\beta}^{\ast}+\bm{\epsilon}_{t}\big),

(S2)

where
\{z_{1,1,t}\}
,
\{z_{1,2,t}\}
,
\{z_{2,1,t}\}
,
\{z_{2,2,t}\}
,
\bm{\mu}^{\ast}
,
\bm{\beta}^{\ast}
,
\mathbf{W}_{1}
,
\mathbf{W}_{2}
, and
\bm{\epsilon}_{t}
are again generated in the same way as described after (
6.1
) in the main text. The covariate
\mathbf{X}_{t}
also has three features, with each entry independently drawn from the standard normal distribution, except that the third feature is made endogenous by adding
m\;\bm{\epsilon}_{t}
. Thus, the parameter
m
controls the level of endogeneity, and we experiment with values of
m
ranging from
0
to
100
. The results for the dimension settings
(T,d)=(50,25)
and
(50,50)
are summarized in Figure
S2
and Figure
S2
, respectively.

Figure S1:
Estimator performance for model (
S2
) with
m
varying from
0
to
100
, under the setting
(T,d)=(50,25)
. The left panel shows the MSE for
\widehat{\bm{\phi}}
(pink),
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
(teal), and
\widehat{\bm{\mu}}
(purple); the right panel shows the specificity (red) and sensitivity (blue) of
\widehat{\bm{\phi}}
. Each value is averaged over 500 repetitions.

Figure S2:
Estimator performance for model (
S2
) with
m
varying from
0
to
100
.

The performance of all estimators improves slightly as the spatial dimension increases, although the general patterns remain consistent across both settings. From the left panels in both figures, we observe that all MSE’s worsen as the covariates become more endogenous. Notably, the performance of the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\mu}}
is most affected by endogeneity, as the estimation errors in
\widehat{\bm{\phi}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
propagate into
\widehat{\bm{\mu}}
. The right panels of both figures show that while the sensitivity of
\widehat{\bm{\phi}}
remains consistently high, its specificity gradually declines as endogeneity increases. Interestingly, the specificity curve exhibits a significant dip when
m
is relatively small, a phenomenon that warrants further investigation in future work.

S2.3
Experiments on the threshold spatial autoregressive models

We present numerical results for the threshold spatial autoregressive model introduced in Section
5.1
. Specifically, we consider the data generating process

\mathbf{y}_{t}=\big(\mathbf{I}_{d}-0.3\,z_{1,t}\mathbf{W}_{1}-0.8\,z_{2,t}\mathbf{W}_{2}\big)^{-1}\big(\bm{\mu}^{\ast}+\mathbf{X}_{t}\bm{\beta}^{\ast}+\bm{\epsilon}_{t}\big),

(S3)

where
\mathbf{W}_{1}
,
\mathbf{W}_{2}
,
\bm{\mu}^{\ast}
,
\mathbf{X}_{t}
,
\bm{\beta}^{\ast}
and
\bm{\epsilon}_{t}
are generated in the same way as in (
6.1
). The indicators
z_{1,t}=\mathbbm{1}\{q_{t}\leq\gamma^{\ast}\}
and
z_{2,t}=\mathbbm{1}\{q_{t}>\gamma^{\ast}\}
are constructed using a threshold variable
q_{t}
and threshold value
\gamma^{\ast}
. Thus, (
S3
) defines a threshold spatial autoregressive model that has changes in both the coefficients and the spatial weight matrices. To simultaneously estimate the parameters and the threshold value, we fit a model of the form

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}_{t}=\Big(\mathbf{I}_{d}-\sum_{l=1}^{19}z_{1,l,t}\phi_{1,l}^{\ast}\mathbf{W}_{1}-\sum_{l=1}^{19}z_{2,l,t}\phi_{2,l}^{\ast}\mathbf{W}_{2}\Big)^{-1}\big(\bm{\mu}^{\ast}+\mathbf{X}_{t}\bm{\beta}^{\ast}+\bm{\epsilon}_{t}\big),\;\;\;\text{where}

(S4)

\displaystyle z_{1,l,t}=\mathbbm{1}\{q_{t}\leq\widehat{\gamma}_{l}\},\;\;\;z_{2,l,t}=\mathbbm{1}\{q_{t}>\widehat{\gamma}_{l}\},\;\;\;\widehat{\gamma}_{l}=\text{$(5\%\cdot l)$-th empirical quantile of $\{q_{t}\}_{t\in[T]}$.}

As discussed in Section
5.1
, we expect
\phi_{1,1}^{\ast},\dots,\phi_{1,19}^{\ast}
to be all zero, except for the one corresponding to
z_{1,l,t}
where
\widehat{\gamma}_{l}
is closest to the true threshold
\gamma^{\ast}
. Let
l^{\ast}
denote the index of this
\widehat{\gamma}_{l}
. Similarly, among
\phi_{2,1}^{\ast},\dots,\phi_{2,19}^{\ast}
, all entries should be zero except for the one corresponding to
z_{2,l^{\ast},t}
. Moreover, we expect
(\phi_{1,l^{\ast}}^{\ast},\phi_{2,l^{\ast}}^{\ast})\approx(0.3,0.8)
.

We consider two types of threshold variables in our experiments:

1.

(AR(5))

q_{t}
follows an AR(5) process with i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
innovations, with
\gamma^{\ast}=0.3
;

2.

(Self-exciting on mean)

q_{t}=\mathbf{1}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}/d
, i.e., the regime switches are driven in a self-exciting manner based on the mean of the previous observation, with
\gamma^{\ast}=1.5
.

Results for
d=50,75
and
T=100,150
are reported in Table
S3
, where the estimators
\widehat{\bm{\phi}}
and
\widehat{\gamma}_{l}
are obtained from fitting the model (
S4
). The evaluation metrics are defined as follows:

\displaystyle\text{$\widehat{\bm{\phi}}$ MSE}:=\text{Mean squared error of $\widehat{\bm{\phi}}$ with $\bm{\phi}$ all zero except for $(\phi_{1,l^{\ast}}^{\ast},\phi_{2,l^{\ast}}^{\ast})=(0.3,0.8)$},

\displaystyle\text{$\widehat{\bm{\phi}}$ True-Unique}:=\mathbbm{1}\{\text{$\widehat{\phi}_{1,l^{\ast}}$ and $\widehat{\phi}_{2,l^{\ast}}$ are uniquely identified as the only nonzero in $\widehat{\bm{\phi}}$}\},

\displaystyle\text{$\widehat{\gamma}_{l}$ MSE}:=\text{Mean squared error of $\widehat{\gamma}_{l}$ relative to the true threshold value $\gamma^{\ast}$}.

The metric
\widehat{\bm{\phi}}
True-Unique is crucial for demonstrating the validity of our algorithm, as it reflects both specificity and sensitivity. In addition, it assesses whether the estimated threshold value is unique. When computing the MSE of
\widehat{\gamma}_{l}
in cases where multiple indices
l
are identified with different
\widehat{\gamma}_{l}
values, we select the index
l
corresponding to the largest
\widehat{\phi}_{1,l^{\ast}}
.

setting

Table S3:
Simulation results for fitting the threshold model (
S4
). The mean and standard deviation (in brackets) of the corresponding error measures over 500 repetitions are reported.

Table
S3
confirms that our procedure can efficiently estimate both the threshold value simultaneously in one go. Although the estimator of
\gamma^{\ast}
is coarse, accurate only up to the
5\%
empirical quantile of the threshold variable, the results show that increasing the data dimensions improves the performance of
\widehat{\gamma}_{l}
. In practice, re-estimation using a finer grid based on such initial threshold estimate could further enhance accuracy.

Comparison with
Li and Lin (2024)

We also compare our method with the quasi-maximum likelihood estimator (QMLE) for the threshold spatial autoregressive model (TSAR) in panel data, which is a variant of the TSAR model in
Li and Lin (2024)
adapted to the time series setup. We consider the model

\mathbf{y}_{t}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\bm{\mu}^{\ast}+(\rho^{\ast}+\varrho^{\ast})\mathbf{W}_{1}\mathbf{y}_{t}+\mathbf{X}_{t}\bm{\beta}^{\ast}+\bm{\epsilon}_{t},&\hbox{$q_{t}\leq\gamma^{\ast}$;}\\
\bm{\mu}^{\ast}+\rho^{\ast}\mathbf{W}_{1}\mathbf{y}_{t}+\mathbf{X}_{t}\bm{\beta}^{\ast}+\bm{\epsilon}_{t},&\hbox{$q_{t}>\gamma^{\ast}$.}\end{array}\right.

(S5)

This model represents a change in regime for the spatial correlation coefficients, from
(\rho^{\ast}+\varrho^{\ast})
to
\rho^{\ast}
, controlled by the threshold variable
\{q_{t}\}
. This setup mirrors the one for change point models in Section
6.2
of the main paper. Specifically, we generate
\bm{\mu}^{\ast}
,
\mathbf{W}_{1}
,
\bm{\mu}^{\ast}
,
\mathbf{X}_{t}
,
\bm{\beta}^{\ast}
and
\bm{\epsilon}_{t}
as described previously in (
S3
), and also generate
q_{t}
using both an AR(5) process and in a self-exciting manner, respectively. We fix
T=100
,
\gamma^{\ast}=0.05
, and
(\rho^{\ast},\varrho^{\ast})=(-0.3,0.3)
, and experiment with three different methods, as described below:

1.

AL: The adaptive LASSO estimator with dynamic variables
\{z_{t,0,1},\dots,z_{t,9,1}\}
, where
z_{t,0,1}=1
,
z_{t,k,1}=\mathbbm{1}\{q_{t}\leq\widehat{\gamma}_{k}\}
for
k\in[9]
,
t\in[100]
, and
\widehat{\gamma}_{k}
is the
(10\%\cdot k)
-th empirical quantile of
\{q_{t}\}_{t\in[100]}
.

2.

AL-DC: The adaptive LASSO estimator using the divide-and-conquer scheme in Remark
1
, with three subsets
\{z_{t,0,1},z_{t,1,1},z_{t,2,1},z_{t,3,1}\}
,
\{z_{t,0,1},z_{t,4,1},z_{t,5,1},z_{t,6,1}\}
, and
\{z_{t,0,1},z_{t,7,1},z_{t,8,1},z_{t,9,1}\}
, where all dynamic variables are the same as in method 1 above. The final estimator is selected from the three results with the smallest estimation error.

3.

QMLE
(
Li and Lin, 2024
)
: The estimation is performed for each
\{\widehat{\gamma}_{k}\}_{k\in[9]}
, where
\widehat{\gamma}_{k}
is the same as in AL. The optimization step is carried out using the Nelder–Mead algorithm, and the final estimator is selected as the one with the smallest estimation error among them.

Table
S4
reports the results for
T=100
and
d=25,50
, where all simulations are repeated 500 times.

.

S2.4
Experiments on the divide-and-conquer scheme in Remark
1

We demonstrate here the numerical performance of the divide-and-conquer scheme depicted in Remark
1
. Under
(T,d)=(100,75)
, consider an extension of (
6.2
) with two change points:

\mathbf{y}_{t}=\big(\mathbf{I}_{d}-0.8\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{t\leq 30\}}\mathbf{W}_{1}+0.9\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{t\leq 60\}}\mathbf{W}_{1}+0.9\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{t>60\}}\mathbf{W}_{2}\big)^{-1}\big(\bm{\mu}^{\ast}+\mathbf{X}_{t}\bm{\beta}^{\ast}+\bm{\epsilon}_{t}\big).

(S6)

That is, the spatial weight matrix changes from
-0.1\mathbf{W}_{1}
to
-0.9\mathbf{W}_{1}
at
t=30
, followed by a change from
-0.9\mathbf{W}_{1}
to
-0.9\mathbf{W}_{2}
at
t=60
. Suppose it is only known a priori that on
\mathcal{T}=\{2,4,\dots,98,100\}
5
5

5

Change point identified at the last observed time point
T=100
represents no change in the structure.
, the spatial weight matrix might change from
\mathbf{W}_{1}
to
\mathbf{W}_{2}
and the spatial correlation coefficients might change as well. We wish to estimate the number of changes and the change locations. Following Remark
1
, we construct the ordered sets
\mathcal{T}_{1}=\{2,4,\dots,20\}
,
\mathcal{T}_{2}=\{20,22,\dots,40\}
,
\mathcal{T}_{3}=\{40,42,\dots,60\}
,
\mathcal{T}_{4}=\{60,62,\dots,80\}
and
\mathcal{T}_{5}=\{80,82,\dots,100\}
. Note that we add “overlaps” between adjacent sets to circumvent falsely identifying change candidates at the margin. Now, the size of each set is
11
, which is reasonable according to the numerical results in Tables
2
. For each
j\in[5]
, consider
6
6

6

Note that the last dynamic variable in
\mathcal{T}_{5}
for
\mathbf{W}_{2}
,
z_{5,2,11,t}
, is 0 for all
t
, so we directly specify
\phi_{5,2,11}^{*}
as 0.

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}_{t}=\Big(\mathbf{I}_{d}-\sum_{l=1}^{|\mathcal{T}_{j}|}z_{j,1,l,t}\phi_{j,1,l}^{\ast}\mathbf{W}_{1}-\sum_{l=1}^{|\mathcal{T}_{j}|}z_{j,2,l,t}\phi_{j,2,l}^{\ast}\mathbf{W}_{2}\Big)^{-1}\big(\bm{\mu}^{\ast}+\mathbf{X}_{t}\bm{\beta}^{\ast}+\bm{\epsilon}_{t}\big),\;\;\;\text{where}

(S7)

\displaystyle z_{j,1,l,t}:=\mathbbm{1}_{\{t\leq(\mathcal{T}_{j})_{l}\}},\;\;\;z_{j,2,l,t}:=\mathbbm{1}_{\{t>(\mathcal{T}_{j})_{l}\}}.

As in Remark
1
, all time points corresponding to nonzero estimates of
\phi_{j,1,l}^{\ast}
or
\phi_{j,2,l}^{\ast}
for
j\in[5],l\in[|\mathcal{T}_{j}|]
are identified and collected to form a refined candidate set
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
, where marginal time points (20, 40, 60,80) are discarded if they are not identified in all
\mathcal{T}_{j}
containing them. Finally, consider

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}_{t}=\Big(\mathbf{I}_{d}-\sum_{l=1}^{|\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}|}z_{1,l,t}\phi_{1,l}^{\ast}\mathbf{W}_{1}-\sum_{l=1}^{|\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}|}z_{2,l,t}\phi_{2,l}^{\ast}\mathbf{W}_{2}\Big)^{-1}\big(\bm{\mu}^{\ast}+\mathbf{X}_{t}\bm{\beta}^{\ast}+\bm{\epsilon}_{t}\big),\;\;\;\text{where}

(S8)

\displaystyle z_{1,l,t}=\mathbbm{1}_{\{t\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{l}\}},\;\;\;z_{2,l,t}=\mathbbm{1}_{\{t>\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{l}\}}.

Then, change points are estimated as the timestamps corresponding to nonzero estimates for
\phi_{1,l}^{\ast}
or
\phi_{2,l}^{\ast}
. The histogram for the estimated change locations over 500 repetitions is shown in the left panel of Figure
S3
and is encouraging. To further quantify the performance of our scheme, we use the Adjusted Rand index (ARI) of the estimated time segmentation against the truth
7
7

7

The estimated time segmentation assigns the same labels to time points between the estimated change points, with different labels assigned after each change point. For true time partitioning with changes at
\{30,60\}
, the intervals
\{1,2,\dots,30\}
,
\{31,32,\dots,60\}
, and
\{61,62,\dots,100\}
are labelled as
1
,
2
, and
3
, respectively.
(
Rand, 1971
;
Hubert and Arabie, 1985
)
, a measure frequently used by change point researchers
(
Wang and Samworth, 2017
)
. The average ARI across all runs is 0.901, again suggesting that our scheme is performing very well.

We also consider (
S6
) under no change or, equivalently, one change at
t=100
:

\mathbf{y}_{t}=\big(\mathbf{I}_{d}+0.9\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\{t\leq T\}}\mathbf{W}_{1}\big)^{-1}\big(\bm{\mu}^{\ast}+\mathbf{X}_{t}\bm{\beta}^{\ast}+\bm{\epsilon}_{t}\big).

(S9)

We follow the same exact procedure to estimate (
S6
), and the histogram for the estimated change points over 500 runs is shown in the right panel of Figure
S3
. In
98\%
of the experiments, exactly
T=100
is identified, meaning no change is detected, which corresponds to a
2\%
false change discovery rate. Furthermore, the average ARI
8
8

8

The true time partition, when there are no changes, labels all time points as 1.
is
0.980
.

Figure S3:
Histograms of estimated change locations under true models (
S6
) (left panel) and (
S9
) (right panel). Both experiments are repeated 500 times.

S3
Additional real data example: enterprise profits

In this case study, we use our proposed model to analyze the total profits of enterprises for a selection of provincial regions in China. Our panel data covers
T=86
monthly periods from March 2016 to August 2024 and 25 provinces and 4 direct-administered municipalities (i.e.,
d=29
).

Due to missingness, we exclude January and February data. The 25 provinces included in the data are Hebei, Shanxi, Inner Mongolia, Liaoning, Jilin, Heilongjiang, Jiangsu, Zhejiang, Anhui, Fujian, Jiangxi, Shandong, Henan, Hubei, Hunan, Guangdong, Guangxi, Hainan, Sichuan, Guizhou, Yunnan, Shaanxi, Gansu, Ningxia, Xinjiang, and the four direct-administered municipalities are Beijing, Tianjin, Shanghai and Chongqing. A snippet of the total profits for August 2024 is shown in Figure
S4
, where the map is produced using the R package
hchinamap
. From the estimation of our null model, it is revealed from
\widehat{\bm{\mu}}
that Guangdong, Beijing, Jiangsu and Shanghai have significantly larger spatial fixed effects than other provinces or municipalities.

Figure S4:
Illustration of the total profits of industrial enterprises within considered Chinese provinces and direct-administered municipalities, in 100 million yuans.

The set of covariates (all standardized) consists of Consumer Price Index (
CPI
), Purchasing Price Index for industrial producers (
PPI
) and output of electricity (
elec
). The data is available at the National Bureau of Statistics of China:
https://data.stats.gov.cn/english/
.

We consider three spatial weight matrix candidates, with each row standardized by its
L_{1}
norm if the row sum exceeds one: inverse distance matrix using inverse of geographical distances between locations computed by the Geodesic WGS-84 System (
\mathbf{W}_{1}
), contiguity matrix (
\mathbf{W}_{2}
), municipality matrix such that all direct-administered municipalities are neighbors (
\mathbf{W}_{3}
).

We treat the covariates as exogenous for two reasons:
CPI
and
PPI
are largely independent of the internal economic activities specific to enterprises within each province or municipality, and electricity supply as a public utility is often price inelastic. Using the aforementioned covariates and spatial weight matrices, we first specify a time-invariant spatial autoregressive model as our null model:

\textit{profit}_{t}=\bm{\mu}^{\ast}+(\phi_{1,0}^{\ast}\mathbf{W}_{1}+\phi_{2,0}^{\ast}\mathbf{W}_{2}+\phi_{3,0}^{\ast}\mathbf{W}_{3})\,\textit{profit}_{t}+(\textit{CPI}_{t},\textit{PPI}_{t},\textit{elec}_{t})\,\bm{\beta}^{\ast}+\bm{\epsilon}_{t}.

(S1)

We estimate the parameters in (
S1
) using our adaptive LASSO estimators. The estimated coefficients
\{\widehat{\phi}_{1,0},\widehat{\phi}_{2,0},\widehat{\phi}_{3,0},\widehat{\bm{\beta}}\}
are presented in Table
S5
, together with the BIC computed according to (
4.2
). The table also shows the standard errors of
\widehat{\phi}_{1,0}
and
\widehat{\phi}_{3,0}
based on Theorem
2
. The respective p-values for testing
\widehat{\phi}_{1,0}=0
and
\widehat{\phi}_{3,0}=0
are both less than
0.0001
, revealing some spillovers among the neighbors of provinces and municipalities. Interestingly,
\widehat{\phi}_{3,0}
suggests a negative spillover effect among the four direct-administered municipalities, which could be explained by that the enterprises within municipalities are main competitors in the market.

\widehat{\phi}_{1,0}

\widehat{\phi}_{2,0}

\widehat{\phi}_{3,0}

\widehat{\beta}_{\textit{CPI}}

\widehat{\beta}_{\textit{PPI}}

\widehat{\beta}_{\textit{elec}}

BIC

Table S5:
Estimated coefficients for model (
S1
), with standard errors (in brackets) computed according to the last part of Section
4
.
\widehat{\beta}_{\textit{CPI}}
,
\widehat{\beta}_{\textit{PPI}}
and
\widehat{\beta}_{\textit{elec}}
denote the estimates of
\bm{\beta}^{\ast}
corresponding to
\textit{CPI}_{t}
,
\textit{PPI}_{t}
and
\textit{elec}_{t}
, respectively.

Hereafter, we refer to (
S1
) as the null model. The rest of the analysis is performed in an exploratory fashion such that we consider spatial autoregressive models of the form (
2.1
) with some
l_{j}
and dynamic variables
\{z_{j,k,t}\}
. We consider the following models:

\begin{split}\text{Model 1}&:\textit{profit}_{t}=\bm{\mu}^{\ast}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\Big(\phi_{j,0}^{\ast}+\sum_{k=1}^{15}\phi_{j,k}^{\ast}\mathbbm{1}\{t\leq 5+5k\}\Big)\mathbf{W}_{j}\,\textit{profit}_{t}+(\textit{CPI}_{t},\textit{PPI}_{t},\textit{elec}_{t})\,\bm{\beta}^{\ast}+\bm{\epsilon}_{t};\\
\text{Model 2}&:\textit{profit}_{t}=\bm{\mu}^{\ast}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\Big(\phi_{j,0}^{\ast}+\sum_{k=1}^{9}\phi_{j,k}^{\ast}\mathbbm{1}\{\text{sd}(\textit{profit}_{t-5})\leq\gamma_{k}\}\Big)\mathbf{W}_{j}\,\textit{profit}_{t}+(\textit{CPI}_{t},\textit{PPI}_{t},\textit{elec}_{t})\,\bm{\beta}^{\ast}+\bm{\epsilon}_{t},\\
&\hskip 6.0pt\text{where }(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{9})=(.177,.193,.202,.207,.217,.219,.231,.250,.302);\\
\text{Model 3}&:\textit{profit}_{t}=\bm{\mu}^{\ast}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\Big(\phi_{j,0}^{\ast}+\sum_{k=1}^{5}\phi_{j,k}^{\ast}\mathbbm{1}\{\text{$t$ divides $2k$}\}\Big)\mathbf{W}_{j}\,\textit{profit}_{t}+(\textit{CPI}_{t},\textit{PPI}_{t},\textit{elec}_{t})\,\bm{\beta}^{\ast}+\bm{\epsilon}_{t};\\
\text{Model 4}&:\textit{profit}_{t}=\bm{\mu}^{\ast}+\Big(\phi_{1,0}^{\ast}\alpha_{3,1}(\mathbf{W}_{1})+\phi_{2,0}^{\ast}\alpha_{3,2}(\mathbf{W}_{1})+\phi_{3,0}^{\ast}\alpha_{3,3}(\mathbf{W}_{1})\Big)\,\textit{profit}_{t}+(\textit{CPI}_{t},\textit{PPI}_{t},\textit{elec}_{t})\,\bm{\beta}^{\ast}+\bm{\epsilon}_{t}.\end{split}

(S2)

Model 1 represents a spatial autoregressive model with the spatial weight matrix potentially changing at
(10,15,\dots,75,80)
. Model 2 is a self-exciting threshold spatial autoregressive model with the standard deviation of
\textit{profit}_{t-5}
as the threshold variable. The sequence of threshold value is in fact the empirical quantile, from
10\%
to
90\%
, of
\text{sd}(\textit{profit}_{t-5})
. Model 3 is similar to the null model but accounts for monthly spillovers for lags of two, four, six, eight and ten months. Model 4 adapts our framework to time-invariant and nonlinear spatial weight matrices, where
\alpha_{3,1}(\mathbf{W}_{1})
,
\alpha_{3,2}(\mathbf{W}_{1})
and
\alpha_{3,3}(\mathbf{W}_{1})
denote the matrices formed by series expansion using the order-3 normalized Laguerre functions (inspired by
Sun (2016)
) based on
\{(\mathbf{W}_{1})_{ij}^{-1}\}_{i,j\in[29]}
.

Table
S6
reports the estimated parameters and BIC for each model. The nonzero
\widehat{\phi}_{1,9}
for Model 1 corresponds to a change in the spillovers featured by
\mathbf{W}_{1}
in March 2020, potentially suggesting inactive economic activities due to COVID-19 starting at the beginning of 2020.

), with standard errors (in brackets) computed according to the last part of Section

. Refer to Table

for the definitions of

,

and

.

Model 2 has the best BIC among all models shown here, including the null model. From Table
S6
, four threshold regions are identified as

\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{t}=\begin{cases}28.718\,\mathbf{W}_{1}-0.522\,\mathbf{W}_{3},&\text{sd}(\textit{profit}_{t-5})\leq 0.202;\\
18.997\,\mathbf{W}_{1}-0.522\,\mathbf{W}_{3},&0.202<\text{sd}(\textit{profit}_{t-5})\leq 0.231;\\

For better illustration, the series of
\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{t}
among Beijing, Shanghai and Guangdong are plotted in Figure
S5
. We see that the spillovers between Beijing and Shanghai is more significant than their respective spillovers with Guangdong.

Figure S5:
Illustration of
\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{t}
of Model 2 in (
S2
) among Beijing, Shanghai and Guangdong, from August 2016 to August 2024.

Figure S6:
Illustration of (time-invariant)
\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{t}
of Model 4 in (
S2
) between Beijing and other provincial regions, against their geographical distances.

On Model 3, Table
S6
suggests that the effect of
\mathbf{W}_{1}
(representing domestic spillovers) remains constant, that of
\mathbf{W}_{2}
(representing more local spillovers) persists every two months but roughly cancels out every half year, and the “municipality spillover” by
\mathbf{W}_{3}
occurs every December. The various spillover patterns featured by the expert spatial weight matrices are intriguing and warrant further investigation.

Model 4 considers a time-invariant spillover effect. An example of the estimated spatial weight matrix is displayed in Figure
S6
. It depicts how the spillovers diminish with the geographical distance. Lastly, the analysis on the total profits data serves to demonstrate our dynamic spatial autoregressive framework. More comprehensive investigations are required to further understand the spatial relations among industrial enterprises in Chinese provinces and direct-administered municipalities.

S4
Proofs of theorems and auxiliary results

S4.1
Lemmas and their proofs

To prove our main theorems, recall first
B_{t,ij}
and
X_{t,ij}
represent the
(i,j)
entry of
\mathbf{B}_{t}
and
\mathbf{X}_{t}
respectively. Define
\mathcal{M}=\bigcap_{i=1}^{13}\mathcal{A}_{i}
, where

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{1}&=\Bigg\{\max_{i,q\in[d]}\max_{j,l\in[r]}\bigg|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}[B_{t,ij}X_{t,ql}-\mathbbm{E}(B_{t,ij}X_{t,ql})]\bigg|<c_{T}\Bigg\},\\
\mathcal{A}_{2}&=\Bigg\{\max_{i,q\in[d]}\max_{j\in[r]}\bigg|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}B_{t,ij}\epsilon_{t,q}\bigg|<c_{T}\Bigg\},\\
\mathcal{A}_{3}&=\Bigg\{\max_{j\in[r]}\bigg|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{q=1}^{d}B_{t,qj}\epsilon_{t,q}\bigg|<c_{T}d^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2w}}\Bigg\},\\
\mathcal{A}_{4}&=\Bigg\{\max_{i\in[d]}\max_{j\in[r]}\big|\bar{B}_{\cdot,ij}-\mathbbm{E}[B_{t,ij}]\big|<c_{T}\Bigg\},\\
\mathcal{A}_{5}&=\Bigg\{\max_{q\in[d]}\big|\bar{\epsilon}_{\cdot,q}\big|<c_{T}\Bigg\},\\
\mathcal{A}_{6}&=\Bigg\{\max_{i\in[d]}\max_{j\in[r]}\big|\bar{X}_{\cdot,ij}\big|<c_{T}\Bigg\},\\
\mathcal{A}_{7}&=\Bigg\{\max_{j\in[r]}\bigg|\sum_{q=1}^{d}\bar{B}_{\cdot,qj}\bar{\epsilon}_{\cdot,q}\bigg|<2^{1/2}c_{T}d^{1/2}\log^{1/2}(T\vee d)S_{\epsilon}(\mu_{b,\text{max}}+c_{T})\Bigg\},\\
\mathcal{A}_{8}&=\Bigg\{\max_{m\in[p]}\max_{n\in[l_{m}]\cup\{0\}}\max_{i,q\in[d]}\max_{j\in[v]}\max_{l\in[r]}\bigg|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}[z_{m,n,t}B_{t,ij}X_{t,ql}-\mathbbm{E}(z_{m,n,t}B_{t,ij}X_{t,ql})]\bigg|<c_{T}\Bigg\},\\
\mathcal{A}_{9}&=\Bigg\{\max_{m\in[p]}\max_{n\in[l_{m}]\cup\{0\}}\max_{i\in[d]}\max_{j\in[r]}\bigg|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}z_{m,n,t}X_{t,ij}\bigg|<c_{T}\Bigg\},\\
\mathcal{A}_{10}&=\Bigg\{\max_{m\in[p]}\max_{n\in[l_{m}]\cup\{0\}}\max_{i,q\in[d]}\max_{j\in[v]}\bigg|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}z_{m,n,t}B_{t,ij}\epsilon_{t,q}\bigg|<c_{T}\Bigg\},\\
\mathcal{A}_{11}&=\Bigg\{\max_{m\in[p]}\max_{n\in[l_{m}]\cup\{0\}}\max_{q\in[d]}\bigg|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}z_{m,n,t}\epsilon_{t,q}\bigg|<c_{T}\Bigg\},\\
\mathcal{A}_{12}&=\Bigg\{\max_{m\in[p]}\max_{n\in[l_{m}]\cup\{0\}}\max_{i\in[d]}\max_{j\in[v]}\bigg|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}[z_{m,n,t}B_{t,ij}-\mathbbm{E}(z_{m,n,t}B_{t,ij})]\bigg|<c_{T}\Bigg\},\\
\mathcal{A}_{13}&=\Bigg\{\max_{m\in[p]}\max_{n\in[l_{m}]\cup\{0\}}\bigg|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}z_{m,n,t}\bigg|<c_{T}\vee z_{\text{max}}\Bigg\},\end{split}

(S1)

where
\bar{B}_{\cdot,ij}:=T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}B_{t,ij}
,
\bar{X}_{\cdot,ij}:=T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}X_{t,ij}
,
\bar{\epsilon}_{\cdot,q}:=T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\epsilon_{t,q}
,
\mu_{b,\text{max}}:=\max_{i,j}\big|\mathbbm{E}[B_{t,ij}]\big|
being a constant implied by Assumption (M1), and
z_{\text{max}}
is the upper bound for
\{z_{j,k,t}\}
implied in (M2) with
z_{\text{max}}=1
for
\{z_{j,0,t}\}
by default. Our main theoretical results depict the properties of estimators on the set
\mathcal{M}
which holds with probability approaching 1 as
T,d\to\infty
by Assumption (R10), as shown in Lemma
2
which is similar to Theorem S.1 of
Lam and Souza (2020)
.

To prove Lemma
2
, we first quote a Nagaev-type inequality for functional dependent data from Theorem 2(ii), 2(iii) and Section 4 of
Liu et al. (2013)
, presented as the following lemma.

Lemma 1

For a zero mean time series process
\mathbf{x}_{t}=\mathbf{f}(\mathcal{F}_{t})
defined in (
3.1
) with dependence measure
\theta_{t,q,i}^{x}
defined in (
3.2
), assume
\Theta_{m,2w}^{x}\leq Cm^{-\alpha}
as in Assumption (M1). Then there exists constants
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
and
C_{3}
independent of
n,\,T
and the index
i
such that

\mathbbm{P}\bigg(\Big|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{t,i}\Big|>n\bigg)\leq\frac{C_{1}T^{w(1/2-\widetilde{\alpha})}}{(Tn)^{w}}+C_{2}\exp(-C_{3}T^{\widetilde{\beta}}n^{2}),

where
\widetilde{\alpha}=\alpha\wedge(1/2-1/w)
and
\widetilde{\beta}=(3+2\widetilde{\alpha}w)/(1+w)
.

Furthermore, assume another zero mean time series process
\{\mathbf{e}_{t}\}
(can be the same process
\{\mathbf{x}_{t}\}
) with
\Theta_{m,2w}^{e}
as in (M1). Then provided
\max_{i}\|x_{t,i}\|_{2w},\,\max_{j}\|e_{t,j}\|_{2w}\leq c_{0}<\infty
where
c_{0}
is a constant, the above Nagaev-type inequality holds for the product process
\{x_{t,i}e_{t,j}-\mathbbm{E}(x_{t,i}e_{t,j})\}
.

The above results also hold for any zero mean non-stationary process
\mathbf{x}_{t}=\mathbf{f}_{t}(\mathcal{F}_{t})
provided that
\max_{i}\|x_{t,i}\|_{w}<\infty
and
\Theta_{m,2w}^{x,\ast}\leq Cm^{-\alpha}
, where
\Theta_{m,q}^{x,\ast}
is uniform tail sum defined as

\Theta_{m,q}^{x,\ast}:=\sum_{t=m}^{\infty}\max_{i}\theta_{t,q,i}^{x,\ast}:=\sum_{t=m}^{\infty}\max_{i}\sup_{t}\big\|x_{t,i}-x_{t,i}^{\prime}\big\|_{q}.

We present Lemma
2
below. Note that we assume
\alpha>1/2-1/w
which can be relaxed at the cost of more complicated rates and longer proofs presented here, and it simplifies the form of Lemma
1
as
w(1/2-\widetilde{\alpha})=\widetilde{\beta}=1
.

Lemma 2

Let Assumptions (M1), (M2) (or (M2’)), and (R2) hold and
\alpha>1/2-1/w
in Assumption (M1). Suppose for the application of the Nagaev-type inequality in Lemma
1
for the processes in
\mathcal{M}=\bigcap_{i=1}^{13}\mathcal{A}_{i}
where
\mathcal{A}_{i}
is defined in (
S1
), the constants
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
and
C_{3}
are the same. Then with
g\geq\sqrt{3/C_{3}}
where
g
is the constant defined in
c_{T}=gT^{-1/2}\log^{1/2}(T\vee d)
, we have

\mathbbm{P}(\mathcal{M})\geq 1-14C_{1}r^{2}vL\Big(\frac{C_{3}}{3}\Big)^{w/2}\frac{d^{2}}{T^{w/2-1}\log^{w/2}(T\vee d)}-\frac{14C_{2}r^{2}vLd^{2}}{T^{3}\vee d^{3}}-\frac{2r}{T\vee d}.

Proof of Lemma
2
.

As shown in Theorem S.1 of
Lam and Souza (2020)
, the tail condition in Assumption (M1) implies
\|\cdot\|_{2w}
is bounded for the processes
\{B_{t,ij}X_{t,ql}-\mathbbm{E}(B_{t,ij}X_{t,ql})\}
,
\{B_{t,ij}\epsilon_{t,q}\}
,
\{X_{t,ij}\}
and
\{\epsilon_{t,q}\}
, and further with Assumption (R2) we have

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}(\mathcal{A}_{1}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}r^{2}\Big(\frac{C_{3}}{3}\Big)^{w/2}\frac{d^{2}}{T^{w/2-1}\log^{w/2}(T\vee d)}+\frac{C_{2}r^{2}d^{2}}{T^{3}\vee d^{3}},

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}(\mathcal{A}_{2}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}r\Big(\frac{C_{3}}{3}\Big)^{w/2}\frac{d^{2}}{T^{w/2-1}\log^{w/2}(T\vee d)}+\frac{C_{2}rd^{2}}{T^{3}\vee d^{3}},

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}(\mathcal{A}_{3}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\Big(\frac{C_{3}}{3}\Big)^{w/2}\frac{r}{T^{w/2-1}\log^{w/2}(T\vee d)}+\frac{C_{2}r}{T^{3}\vee d^{3}},

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}(\mathcal{A}_{4}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}r\Big(\frac{C_{3}}{3}\Big)^{w/2}\frac{d}{T^{w/2-1}\log^{w/2}(T\vee d)}+\frac{C_{2}rd}{T^{3}\vee d^{3}},

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}(\mathcal{A}_{5}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\Big(\frac{C_{3}}{3}\Big)^{w/2}\frac{d}{T^{w/2-1}\log^{w/2}(T\vee d)}+\frac{C_{2}d}{T^{3}\vee d^{3}},

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}(\mathcal{A}_{6}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}r\Big(\frac{C_{3}}{3}\Big)^{w/2}\frac{d}{T^{w/2-1}\log^{w/2}(T\vee d)}+\frac{C_{2}rd}{T^{3}\vee d^{3}},

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}(\mathcal{A}_{7}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2r}{T\vee d}+\mathbbm{P}(\mathcal{A}_{4}^{c})+\mathbbm{P}(\mathcal{A}_{5}^{c}).

Consider the remaining sets. First let Assumption (M2) hold and it is easy to see
\|\cdot\|_{2w}
is bounded for the processes
\{z_{m,n,t}B_{t,ij}X_{t,ql}-\mathbbm{E}(z_{m,n,t}B_{t,ij}X_{t,ql})\}
,
\{z_{m,n,t}B_{t,ij}\epsilon_{t,q}\}
,
\{z_{m,n,t}X_{t,ij}\}
and
\{z_{m,n,t}\epsilon_{t,q}\}
, and their uniform tail sums satisfy the condition in Lemma
1
. Thus, apply Lemma
1
first on
\mathcal{A}_{8}^{c}
and we have by the union bound,

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}(\mathcal{A}_{8}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m\in[p]}\sum_{n\in[l_{m}]}\sum_{i,q\in[d]}\sum_{j\in[v]}\sum_{l\in[r]}\mathbbm{P}\Bigg(\bigg|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}[z_{m,n,t}B_{t,ij}X_{t,ql}-\mathbbm{E}(z_{m,n,t}B_{t,ij}X_{t,ql})]\bigg|\geq c_{T}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq d^{2}rvL\Big(\frac{C_{1}T}{(Tc_{T})^{w}}+C_{2}\exp(-C_{3}Tc_{T}^{2})\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}rvL\Big(\frac{C_{3}}{3}\Big)^{w/2}\frac{d^{2}}{T^{w/2-1}\log^{w/2}(T\vee d)}+\frac{C_{2}rvLd^{2}}{T^{3}\vee d^{3}}.

Similarly using Lemma
1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}(\mathcal{A}_{9}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}rL\Big(\frac{C_{3}}{3}\Big)^{w/2}\frac{d}{T^{w/2-1}\log^{w/2}(T\vee d)}+\frac{C_{2}rLd}{T^{3}\vee d^{3}},

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}(\mathcal{A}_{10}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}vL\Big(\frac{C_{3}}{3}\Big)^{w/2}\frac{d^{2}}{T^{w/2-1}\log^{w/2}(T\vee d)}+\frac{C_{2}vLd^{2}}{T^{3}\vee d^{3}},

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}(\mathcal{A}_{11}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}L\Big(\frac{C_{3}}{3}\Big)^{w/2}\frac{d}{T^{w/2-1}\log^{w/2}(T\vee d)}+\frac{C_{2}Ld}{T^{3}\vee d^{3}},

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}(\mathcal{A}_{12}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}vL\Big(\frac{C_{3}}{3}\Big)^{w/2}\frac{d}{T^{w/2-1}\log^{w/2}(T\vee d)}+\frac{C_{2}vLd}{T^{3}\vee d^{3}},

while
\mathbbm{P}(\mathcal{A}_{13}^{c})=0
by Assumption (M2). On the other hand, if we have Assumption (M2’), the above results remain valid, except that

\mathbbm{P}(\mathcal{A}_{13}^{c})\leq C_{1}L\Big(\frac{C_{3}}{3}\Big)^{w/2}\frac{1}{T^{w/2-1}\log^{w/2}(T\vee d)}+\frac{C_{2}L}{T^{3}\vee d^{3}},

by applying Lemma
1
given the bounded support and tail sum assumption in (M2’). For any
\{z_{j,0,t}\}
, we may treat it as a non-stochastic basis as in (M2) and the result follows. Lastly, by
\mathbbm{P}(\mathcal{M})\geq 1-\sum_{i=1}^{13}\mathbbm{P}(\mathcal{A}_{i}^{c})
we complete the proof of Lemma
2
.

\square

We present the following lemma with a short proof as well, and we will frequently utilize the defined notation
\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{H},K}
(which is the same definition in Theorem
2
) in the proof of main theorems.

Lemma 3

For any
n\times d
matrix
\mathbf{H}=(\mathbf{h}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{h}_{n})^{\top}
and any
d\times K
matrix
\mathbf{M}
, define

\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{H},K}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{K}\otimes\mathbf{h}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{I}_{K}\otimes\mathbf{h}_{n}\end{pmatrix}.

We then have
\mathbf{H}\mathbf{M}=\Big(\mathbf{I}_{n}\otimes\textnormal{vec}(\mathbf{M})^{\top}\Big)\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{H},K}
.

Proof of Lemma
3
.

Notice that

\Big(\mathbf{I}_{n}\otimes\textnormal{vec}(\mathbf{M})^{\top}\Big)\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{H},K}=\begin{pmatrix}\text{vec}\big(\mathbf{M}\big)^{\top}(\mathbf{I}_{K}\otimes\mathbf{h}_{1})\\
\vdots\\
\text{vec}\big(\mathbf{M}\big)^{\top}(\mathbf{I}_{K}\otimes\mathbf{h}_{n})\end{pmatrix}.

The
j
-th row of this matrix (as a column vector) is hence

(\mathbf{I}_{K}\otimes\mathbf{h}_{j}^{\top})\text{vec}\big(\mathbf{M}\big)=\text{vec}\big(\mathbf{h}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{M}\big)=\text{vec}\big(\mathbf{M}^{\top}\mathbf{h}_{j}\big)=\mathbf{M}^{\top}\mathbf{h}_{j},

which is the
j
-th row of
\mathbf{H}\mathbf{M}
indeed.
\square

Remark 2

With the notation of
\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{H},K}
, we may write any
d\times K
matrix
\mathbf{M}
as

\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{I}_{d}\mathbf{M}=\Big(\mathbf{I}_{d}\otimes\textnormal{vec}(\mathbf{M})^{\top}\Big)\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{I}_{d},K},

which will be useful if we are interested in the interaction only between
\mathbf{A},\,\mathbf{M}
in
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{M}
, with
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbbm{R}^{l\times r}
and
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbbm{R}^{r\times d}
, since we have

\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{M}=(\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{\top})^{\top}\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{B}^{\top},l}^{\top}\Big(\mathbf{I}_{d}\otimes\textnormal{vec}(\mathbf{A}^{\top})\Big)\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{B}^{\top},l}^{\top}\Big(\mathbf{I}_{d}\otimes\textnormal{vec}(\mathbf{A}^{\top})\textnormal{vec}(\mathbf{M})^{\top}\Big)\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{I}_{d},K}.

Moreover, notice that in Lemma
3
, if
K=1
, i.e.
\mathbf{M}
is a vector, then
\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{H},1}=\textnormal{vec}(\mathbf{H}^{\top})
and Lemma
3
simply coincides with the fact that

\mathbf{B}\mathbf{M}=\textnormal{vec}(\mathbf{M}^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\top})=\textnormal{vec}(\mathbf{M}^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{I}_{r})=\big(\mathbf{I}_{r}\otimes\mathbf{M}^{\top}\big)\textnormal{vec}(\mathbf{B}^{\top})=\Big(\mathbf{I}_{r}\otimes\textnormal{vec}(\mathbf{M})^{\top}\Big)\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{B},1}.

Thus, we can interpret
\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{H},K}
to be a “
K
-block vectorization” of
\mathbf{H}
, as a generalization of vectorization.

Lemma 4

Let the assumptions in Theorem
2
hold. Let
\mathbf{R}_{\beta}
and
\bm{\Sigma}_{\beta}
be defined in Theorem
2
. For
I_{2}=\big[\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{t})\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{B}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{t})\big]^{-1}T^{-2}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\bm{\epsilon}^{\nu}
, we have
I_{2}
asymptotically normal with rate
T^{-1/2}d^{-(1-b)/2}
such that

T^{1/2}(\mathbf{R}_{\beta}\bm{\Sigma}_{\beta}\mathbf{R}_{\beta}^{\top})^{-1/2}I_{2}\xrightarrow{D}\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{r}).

Proof of Lemma
4
.

Given any non-zero
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbbm{R}^{r}
with
\|\bm{\alpha}\|_{1}\leq c<\infty
, we construct below the asymptotic normality of
\bm{\alpha}^{\top}I_{2}
which is
T^{1/2}d^{(1-b)/2}
-convergent. First, we can further decompose

\displaystyle\bm{\alpha}^{\top}I_{2}

\displaystyle=\big[\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{t})\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{B}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{t})\big]^{-1}\big(T^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\nu}-\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{t})\big)T^{-1}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\bm{\epsilon}^{\nu}

\displaystyle\hskip 12.0pt+\big[\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{t})\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{B}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{t})\big]^{-1}\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{t})T^{-1}(\mathbf{B}^{\nu})^{\top}\bm{\epsilon}^{\nu},

with the second term dominating the first by (
S6
). Recall that

\mathbf{R}_{\beta}=\big[\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{t})\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{B}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{t})\big]^{-1}\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{t}),

then

\bm{\alpha}^{\top}I_{2}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\bm{\alpha}^{\top}\mathbf{R}_{\beta}(\mathbf{B}_{t}-\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{B}_{t}))^{\top}\bm{\epsilon}_{t}(1+o_{P}(1)).

To construct the asymptotic normality of
\bm{\alpha}^{\top}I_{2}
, we want to show the following as in (
S33
) that

\sum_{t\geq 0}\Big\|P_{0}(\bm{\alpha}^{\top}\mathbf{R}_{\beta}(\mathbf{B}_{t}-\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{B}_{t}))^{\top}\bm{\epsilon}_{t})\Big\|_{2}<\infty,

(S2)

so that Theorem 3 (ii) of
Wu (2011)
can then be applied. With the definition
s_{2}:=\bm{\alpha}^{\top}\mathbf{R}_{\beta}\bm{\Sigma}_{\beta}\mathbf{R}_{\beta}^{\top}\bm{\alpha}
, we have
T^{1/2}s_{2}^{-1/2}\bm{\alpha}^{\top}I_{2}\xrightarrow{D}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and hence

T^{1/2}(\mathbf{R}_{\beta}\bm{\Sigma}_{\beta}\mathbf{R}_{\beta}^{\top})^{-1/2}I_{2}\xrightarrow{D}\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{r}).

(S3)

Similar to the proof of (
S33
), we have (
S2
) hold by Assumption (R7) and

\displaystyle\hskip 12.0pt\Big\|P_{0}(\bm{\alpha}^{\top}\mathbf{R}_{\beta}(\mathbf{B}_{t}-\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{B}_{t}))^{\top}\bm{\epsilon}_{t})\Big\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\Big\|\bm{\alpha}^{\top}\mathbf{R}_{\beta}\big\{P_{0}((\mathbf{B}_{t}-\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{B}_{t}))^{\top})\mathbbm{E}_{0}(\bm{\epsilon}_{t})\big\}+\bm{\alpha}^{\top}\mathbf{R}_{\beta}\big\{\mathbbm{E}_{-1}((\mathbf{B}_{t}-\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{B}_{t}))^{\top})P_{0}(\bm{\epsilon}_{t})\big\}\Big\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\Big\{2\bm{\alpha}^{\top}\mathbf{R}_{\beta}\mathbbm{E}\Big\{P_{0}((\mathbf{B}_{t}-\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{B}_{t}))^{\top})\,\mathbbm{E}_{0}(\bm{\epsilon}_{t})\,\mathbbm{E}_{0}(\bm{\epsilon}_{t}^{\top})\,P_{0}(\mathbf{B}_{t}-\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{B}_{t}))\Big\}\mathbf{R}_{\beta}^{\top}\bm{\alpha}\Big\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 12.0pt+\Big\{2\bm{\alpha}^{\top}\mathbf{R}_{\beta}\mathbbm{E}\Big\{\mathbbm{E}_{-1}((\mathbf{B}_{t}-\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{B}_{t}))^{\top})\,P_{0}(\bm{\epsilon}_{t})\,P_{0}(\bm{\epsilon}_{t}^{\top})\,\mathbbm{E}_{-1}(\mathbf{B}_{t}-\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{B}_{t}))\Big\}\mathbf{R}_{\beta}^{\top}\bm{\alpha}\Big\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle=O\Big(\big\|\bm{\alpha}\big\|_{1}\big\|\mathbf{R}_{\beta}\big\|_{\infty}\Big)\Big(d\cdot\max_{j\in[d]}\mathbbm{E}^{1/2}(\mathbbm{E}_{0}^{2}(\epsilon_{t,j}))\,\max_{j\in[d]}\max_{k\in[v]}\big\|P_{0}(B_{t,jk})\big\|_{2}+d\cdot\sigma_{\text{max}}\max_{j\in[d]}\big\|P_{0}(\epsilon_{t,j})\big\|_{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=O\Big(\max_{j\in[d]}\big\|P_{0}^{\epsilon}(\epsilon_{t,j})\big\|_{2}+\max_{j\in[d]}\max_{k\in[v]}\big\|P_{0}^{b}(B_{t,jk})\big\|_{2}\Big),

where the second last equality used Assumption (R2), and the last used
\big\|\mathbf{R}_{\beta}\big\|_{\infty}=O(d^{-2}\cdot d)=O(d^{-1})
by (
S4
) and Assumption (R3).

It remains to show
\bm{\alpha}^{\top}I_{2}
is indeed of order
T^{-1/2}d^{-(1-b)/2}
. To this end, we only need to show
s_{2}
is of order
d^{-(1-b)}
. First, we have
\mathbf{R}_{\beta}\mathbf{R}_{\beta}^{\top}=\big[\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{t})\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{B}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{t})\big]^{-1}
which has all eigenvalues of order
d^{-2}
from (
S4
) and Assumption (R3). Consider any
j
-th diagonal element of
\bm{\Sigma}_{\beta}
, we have

\displaystyle(\bm{\Sigma}_{\beta})_{jj}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau}\mathbbm{E}\big\{(\mathbf{B}_{t}-\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{B}_{t}))_{\cdot j}^{\top}\bm{\epsilon}_{t}\bm{\epsilon}_{t+\tau}^{\top}(\mathbf{B}_{t+\tau}-\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{B}_{t}))_{\cdot j}\big\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau}\text{tr}\big\{\mathbbm{E}((\mathbf{B}_{t+\tau}-\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{B}_{t}))_{\cdot j}(\mathbf{B}_{t+\tau}-\mathbbm{E}(\mathbf{B}_{t}))_{\cdot j}^{\top})\,\mathbbm{E}(\bm{\epsilon}_{t}\bm{\epsilon}_{t+\tau}^{\top})\big\},

which is finite and has order exactly
d^{1+b}
by Assumption (R8). Notice
\bm{\Sigma}_{\beta}
is
r\times r
, the order of eigenvalues of
\bm{\Sigma}_{\beta}
is hence exactly
d^{1+b}
. The order of
s_{2}
is
d^{b-1}
by

\|\bm{\alpha}\|_{1}^{2}\lambda_{\text{min}}(\mathbf{R}_{\beta}\mathbf{R}_{\beta}^{\top})\lambda_{\text{min}}(\bm{\Sigma}_{\beta})\leq s_{2}\leq\|\bm{\alpha}\|_{1}^{2}\lambda_{\text{max}}(\mathbf{R}_{\beta}\mathbf{R}_{\beta}^{\top})\lambda_{\text{max}}(\bm{\Sigma}_{\beta}).

This completes the proof of Lemma
4
.

\square

Proofs of corollaries
